.

As the last piece of notation, we introduce the subgroups

{\rm Br}_{1}({\cal C}^{(d)})_{s}\subset{\rm Br}({\cal C}^{(d)})_{s}\text{ and }{\rm Br}_{1}({\rm Pic}^{d}({\cal C}/B))_{s}\subset{\rm Br}({\rm Pic}^{d}({\cal C}/B))_{s}

of all elements that are trivial on the geometric fibres.

Lemma 3.1
.

The map
{\rm Br}({\cal C})\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces{\rm Br}({\cal C}^{(g)})
,
\alpha\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@stopper}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\alpha^{(g)}\coloneqq\prod_{i=1}^{g}{\rm pr}_{i}^{\ast}\alpha
induces an isomorphism

{\rm Br}({\cal C})_{s}\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 1.375pt\raise 4.34428pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.34428pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\sim}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces{\rm Br}_{1}({\cal C}^{(g)})_{s}\simeq{\rm Br}_{1}({\rm Pic}^{g}({\cal C}/B))_{s}.

Proof.

The proof follows ideas in
[
9
,
35
]
for curves over algebraically closed fields.
To simplify the notation we shall write
{\rm Pic}^{d}
for
{\rm Pic}^{d}({\cal C}/B)
throughout.

The first step is to show that the pull-back under the natural morphism

{\cal C}\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces{\rm Pic}^{1}\simeq{\rm Pic}^{0},~x\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@stopper}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces{\cal O}(x)\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@stopper}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces{\cal O}(x-s)

induces isomorphisms

{\rm Br}({\cal C})\simeq{\rm Br}_{1}({\rm Pic}^{0})\text{ and }{\rm Br}({\cal C})_{s}\simeq{\rm Br}_{1}({\rm Pic}^{0})_{s}.

Indeed, the restriction
map
{\rm Br}_{1}({\rm Pic}^{0}))\subset{\rm Br}({\rm Pic}^{0})\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces{\rm Br}({\cal C})
is compatible with the Leray spectral sequence for the two projections. Since for dimension reasons, the projection

f\colon{\cal C}\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces B
satisfies
H^{0}(B,R^{2}f_{\ast}\mathbb{G}_{m})=0
, we
have
{\rm Br}_{1}({\cal C})={\rm Br}({\cal C})
. Thus, there is a commutative diagram

\textstyle{{\rm Br}(B)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{=}
\textstyle{{\rm Br}_{1}({\rm Pic}^{1})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H^{1}(B,R^{1}\check{f}_{\ast}\mathbb{G}_{m})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\textstyle{H^{3}(B,\mathbb{G}_{m})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{=}
\textstyle{{\rm Br}(B)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\rm Br}({\cal C})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H^{1}(B,R^{1}f_{\ast}\mathbb{G}_{m})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H^{3}(B,\mathbb{G}_{m}),}

which is enough to prove the claim.
Note that the injectivity of the pull-back of
{\rm Br}(B)
to the Brauer groups of
{\cal C}
and
{\rm Pic}^{1}

is a consequences of the existence of a section and the vertical isomorphism follows from the
isomorphism
R^{1}\check{f}_{\ast}\mathbb{G}_{m}\simeq R^{1}f_{\ast}\mathbb{G}_{m}
.

In the second step we use the pull-backs under the morphisms

{\cal C}^{(d)}\,\,\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@hook{1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces{\cal C}^{(d+1)},~(x_{1},\ldots,x_{d})\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@stopper}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces(x_{1},\ldots,x_{d},s)

and

{\rm Pic}^{d}\,\,\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@hook{1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces{\rm Pic}^{d+1},~L\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@stopper}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces L\otimes{\cal O}(s),

to construct the commutative diagram

\textstyle{{\rm Br}({\cal C})_{s}}
\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\rm Br}_{1}({\cal C}^{(2)})}
\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\cdots}
\textstyle{\cdots}
\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\rm Br}_{1}({\cal C}^{(g)})_{s}}
\textstyle{{\rm Br}_{1}({\rm Pic}^{1})_{s}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\rm Br}_{1}({\rm Pic}^{2})_{s}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\cdots}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\textstyle{\cdots}
\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\rm Br}_{1}({\rm Pic}^{g})_{s}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces.}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}

To conclude, observe that the composition in the upper row is split by
\alpha\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@stopper}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\alpha^{(g)}
. Indeed, if
\alpha
restricts trivially to the section, then the restriction of the
d
-fold exterior product
\alpha^{\times d+1}=\alpha\times\cdots\times\alpha\times\alpha
to
{\cal C}\times\cdots\times{\cal C}\times\{s\}
equals the
d
-fold
exterior product
\alpha^{\times d}=\alpha\times\cdots\times\alpha
.
∎

The important part for us is the surjectivity, i.e. any
\beta\in{\rm Br}_{1}({\rm Pic}^{0}({\cal C}/B))_{s}
is
of the form
\alpha^{(g)}
for some
\alpha\in{\rm Br}({\cal C})_{s}
. Here, we use the identification

{\rm Pic}^{0}\simeq{\rm Pic}^{g}
. Without it, the next result would need to be phrased for classes on
{\rm Pic}^{g}
.

Proposition 3.2
.

Assume
{\cal C}\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces B
is a family of smooth projective curves of genus
g

with a section. Then for any Brauer class
\beta=\alpha^{(g)}\in{\rm Br}_{1}({\rm Pic}^{0}({\cal C}/B))_{s}

with
\alpha\in{\rm Br}({\cal C})_{s}
one has

{\rm ind}(\beta)\mid{\rm ind}(\alpha)^{g}.

In particular, if
{\rm ind}(\alpha)={\rm per}(\alpha)
,
then

{\rm ind}(\beta)\mid{\rm per}(\beta)^{g}.

Proof.

Assume
{\cal A}
is an Azumaya algebra on
{\cal C}
that represents
\alpha
. Then the exterior
product
{\cal B}\coloneqq{\cal A}\boxtimes\cdots\boxtimes{\cal A}
on
{\cal C}^{n}\coloneqq{\cal C}\times_{B}\cdots\times_{B}{\cal C}
represents
the class
\alpha^{\times n}
on
{\cal C}^{n}
which descends to the class
\beta=\alpha^{(n)}
on
{\cal C}^{(n)}
.

The algebra
{\cal B}
, which satisfies
{\rm rk}({\cal B})={\rm rk}({\cal A})^{n}
, comes with a natural linearisation with respect to the action of
{\mathfrak{S}}_{n}
. This action is free on the complement
of the big diagonal
\{(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})\mid\exists i\neq j:x_{i}=x_{j}\}
. Hence,
{\cal B}
descends to an Azumaya algebra on an open subset
U\subset{\cal C}^{(n)}
that represents the restriction
\beta|_{U}
.
The gcd of the
{\rm rk}({\cal A})^{1/2}
over all
{\cal A}
representing
{\cal A}
computes
{\rm ind}(\alpha)

and hence
{\rm ind}(\beta)
divides the gcd of all
{\rm rk}({\cal A})^{n/2}
.
∎

In contrast to the discussion in §§​
1
&
2
, the arguments in this section do not proceed by constructing sheaves on some twist
A_{\beta}
and then translating those back to
\beta
-twisted sheaves on the original variety, which here is
{\rm Pic}^{0}({\cal C}/B)
.
So in this sense, the arguments are much more direct.

3.2.
Period-index for general projective varieties

This short section is an interlude
on general projective varieties. We briefly explain how to apply the techniques in §​
2.5
to improve upon the result in
[
29
]
. As the
improvement is minor and far from being optimal, with an exponent still depending on the geometry of
X
and not just its dimension, we will only sketch the main idea.

Recall that one of the main results in
[
29
]
asserted that

{\rm ind}(\alpha)\mid{\rm per}(\alpha)^{2g}
for all Brauer classes
\alpha\in{\rm Br}(X)
on an arbitrary projective variety
X
with
g
being the genus of a very ample complete intersection curve.
We claim that the techniques developed here now prove the better bound:

{\rm ind}(\alpha)\mid{\rm per}(\alpha)^{g}.

Indeed, the proof in
[
29
]
uses the existence of a family

\textstyle{{\cal C}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\,\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{h}
\textstyle{\bar{\cal C}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{X}
\textstyle{B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathbb{P}}

with
\mathbb{P}
a projective space of dimension
\dim(X)-1
, the projection
{\cal C}\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces X
being
a blow-up, and
\bar{\cal C}\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\mathbb{P}
a family of complete intersection curves with its
smooth part
{\cal C}\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces B
. Also note that
\bar{\cal C}\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\mathbb{P}
comes with a section, the pre-image of
a closed point of
X
contained in the center of the blow-up, which trivialises all Brauer classes
coming from
X
.

The dual fibration
{\rm Pic}^{0}({\cal C}/B)\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces B
is principally polarised and we pick one such
principal polarisation
L
. If
\alpha\in{\rm Br}(X)
is of period
r
, then
L^{r}
glues
to a line bundle on the twist
{\rm Pic}_{\alpha}^{0}({\cal C}/B)
if the obstruction
o(\alpha,L^{r})\in{\rm Br}(B)

vanishes. The obstruction class is the image under the boundary map

H^{1}(B,R^{1}h_{\ast}\mu_{r})\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces H^{2}(B,\mathbb{G}_{m})
of a certain natural class

\alpha^{\prime}\in H^{1}(B,R^{1}h_{\ast}\mu_{r})
induced by
\alpha
. It then suffices to show that
\alpha^{\prime}
extends
to a class in
H^{1}(\mathbb{P},{\cal K}_{r})
with an appropriately defined sheaf
{\cal K}_{r}
and that
there exists an extension of
{\cal K}_{r}
by
\mathbb{G}_{m}
on
\mathbb{P}
that extends the given one
on
B
. All this can be achieved and then
o(\alpha,L^{r})\in H^{2}(B,\mathbb{G}_{m})
is the image
of a class under
H^{2}(\mathbb{P},\mathbb{G}_{m})\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces H^{2}(B,\mathbb{G}_{m})
and, therefore, trivial, for
\mathbb{P}
is a projective
space.

The arguments in §​
2
go indeed through and are at places even easier. For example,
working with a covering family of curves makes it unnecessary to restrict to certain
finite index subgroups of
{\rm Br}(X)
. Indeed, all classes
\alpha\in{\rm Br}(X)
immediately
lead to twists
{\rm Pic}_{\alpha}^{0}({\cal C}/B)
. Hence, all coprimality assumptions on
{\rm per}(\alpha)
can
be avoided.

3.3.
Moduli spaces of one-dimensional sheaves on K3 surfaces

As an application we mention the case of moduli spaces of one-dimensional
sheaves on K3 surfaces. For more details and references concerning moduli
spaces of sheaves on K3 surfaces we refer to
[
26
, Ch. 10.3]
or
[
28
, Ch. 6]
.

We consider a complex projective K3 surface
S
together with an ample linear system
|H|
of genus
g
, i.e.
(H.H)=2g-2
. Then we let

X\coloneqq M(v)

be the moduli space of stable sheaves on
S
with Mukai vector
v=(0,H,1)
. In other words,
M(v)
generically parametrises line bundles of degree
g
on curves
C\in|H|
. Stability is taken with respect to a generic polarisation which implies that
X
is a projective hyperkähler manifold of dimension
2g
and the support
map describes a Lagrangian fibration
X\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces|H|
.

Furthermore, there exists a universal family on
S\times X
which is known to produce
a Hodge isometry
\tilde{H}(S,\mathbb{Z})\supset v^{\perp}\simeq H^{2}(X,\mathbb{Z})
and, as a consequence,
an isomorphism
{\rm Br}(S)\simeq{\rm Br}(X)
via the description of the Brauer group in terms of the exponential sequence.

The next result is a variant of Theorem
0.3
for Lagrangian fibrations
but without any coprimality condition on the period.

Corollary 3.3
.

For every class
\alpha\in{\rm Br}(X)
one has

{\rm ind}(\alpha)\mid{\rm per}(\alpha)^{g}.

Proof.

Consider the universal family of smooth curves
{\cal C}\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces B\coloneqq|H|_{\text{sm}}
.
Then
{\rm Pic}^{g}({\cal C}/B)
is an open subset of
X
compatible with the projection
X\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces|H|
.

We claim that the composition

\textstyle{{\rm Br}(S)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sim}
\textstyle{{\rm Br}(X)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\rm Br}({\rm Pic}^{g}({\cal C}/B))}

of the above isomorphism
{\rm Br}(S)\simeq{\rm Br}(X)
with the restriction
to the open subset
{\rm Pic}^{g}({\cal C}/B)\subset X
is nothing but the map
\alpha\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@stopper}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\alpha^{(g)}
in Lemma
3.1
. Then Proposition
3.2
would immediately imply the assertion. The assertion follows from the discussion in the next section and the fact that
the two isomorphisms
{\rm Br}(S)\simeq{\rm Br}(X)
and
{\rm Br}(S)\simeq{\rm Br}(S^{[n]})
are compatible with
the birational identification of
S^{[g]}
and
X
.
∎

As the Hilbert scheme
S^{[g]}
is in fact birational to
{\cal C}^{(g)}
and hence to
{\rm Pic}^{g}({\cal C}/B)
, it can also be seen as a special case
of Theorem
0.4
or, equivalently, Corollary
3.7
below.

Remark 3.4
.

The other moduli spaces
M(v)
with
v=(0,H,d+1-g)
can be treated similarly by working with
the Abel–Jacobi map
{\cal C}^{(d)}\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces{\rm Pic}^{d}({\cal C}/B)
with
d\geq g
. However, the exponent becomes worse, namely
{\rm per}(\alpha)^{d}
instead of
{\rm per}(\alpha)^{g}
.
But one can always reduce to
2g-2\geq d

Remark 3.5
.

According to
[
30
, Thm. 1.2]
, every Lagrangian fibration of a hyperkähler manifold
of
{\text{K}3}^{[n]}
-type is birational to some relative twisted
Picard scheme
{\rm Pic}_{\gamma}^{0}({\cal C}/|H|)\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces|H|
. Generalising
Proposition
3.2
to the twisted case would lead to a stronger version of
Theorem
0.3
for all Lagrangian fibrations of
{\text{K}3}^{[n]}
-type with no
(or weaker) coprimality assumptions on
{\rm per}(\alpha)
.

3.4.
Hilbert schemes of K3 surfaces

For a K3 surface
S
, we denote by
S^{(n)}
its symmetric product and by
S^{[n]}

its Hilbert scheme of subschemes of length
n
. The Hilbert scheme can be viewed as a resolution
of the singular symmetric product via the Hilbert–Chow morphism
\pi\colon S^{[n]}\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces S^{(n)}
.

We will use the Hodge theoretic description of the Brauer group and so have to work over
the complex number. Thus, from now on,
S
denotes a complex projective K3 surface. For fixed
n-1
pairwise distinct points
x_{1},\ldots,x_{n-1}\in S
the map
x\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@stopper}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\{x_{1},\ldots,x_{n-1},x\}
extends to an embedding

(3.2)

\hat{S}\coloneqq\text{Bl}_{\{x_{i}\}}(S)\,\,\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@hook{1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces S^{[n]}.

Lemma 3.6
.

The pull-back under the embedding
(
3.2
)
induces a primitive embedding
of Hodge structures

H^{2}(S^{[n]},\mathbb{Z})\,\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@hook{1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces H^{2}(\hat{S},\mathbb{Z})

and an isomorphism

(3.3)

{\rm Br}(S^{[n]})\simeq{\rm Br}(\hat{S})\simeq{\rm Br}(S).

The inverse of this isomorphism is given by

(3.4)

{\rm Br}(S)\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces{\rm Br}(S^{(n)})\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces{\rm Br}(S^{[n]}),~\alpha\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@stopper}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\alpha^{(n)}\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@stopper}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\alpha^{[n]}\coloneqq\pi^{\ast}\alpha^{(n).}

Proof.

According to
[
7
, §​ 6]
, there exists an isomorphism
H^{2}(S^{[n]},\mathbb{Z})\simeq H^{2}(S,\mathbb{Z})\oplus\mathbb{Z}\delta
. Here,
2\delta
is the class of the exceptional divisor
of the Hilbert–Chow morphism
\pi\colon S^{[n]}\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces S^{(n)}
and the inclusion
i\colon H^{2}(S,\mathbb{Z})\,\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@hook{1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces H^{2}(S^{[n]},\mathbb{Z})
is given by the condition that
i(a)
is the pull-back
under
\pi
of the class on the symmetric product
S^{(n)}
that pulls back to
\sum{\rm pr}_{i}^{\ast}a\in H^{2}(S^{n},\mathbb{Z})
under the quotient map
S^{n}\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces S^{(n)}
.

Observe that composing
i
with the pull-back under (
3.2
) is simply the
natural embedding
H^{2}(S,\mathbb{Z})\,\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@hook{1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces H^{2}(\hat{S},\mathbb{Z})
. To prove the first assertion, it then suffices to remark that the exceptional class
\delta
restricts to
the sum
\sum[E_{i}]
of the exceptional curves of the blow-up
\hat{S}\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces S
, which is a primitive class in
H^{2}(\hat{S},\mathbb{Z})
.

In order to prove (
3.3
), we use that
{\rm Br}(S)
is naturally identified with the torsion of
the analytic Brauer group

H^{2}(S,{\cal O}_{S}^{\ast})\simeq H^{2}(S,{\cal O})/\operatorname{im}(H^{2}(S,\mathbb{Z})\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces H^{2}(S,{\cal O}))
and similarly for
S^{[n]}
. Then use again the above isomorphism of Hodge structures which in particular induces

H^{1,1}(S^{[n]},\mathbb{Z})\simeq H^{1,1}(S,\mathbb{Z})
.

For (
3.4
), we imitate the arguments in §​
3.1
for families of curves. It suffices to observe that the composition of (
3.4
) with (
3.3
) is the identity.
∎

Then Theorem
0.4
is an immediate consequence and we state it again as:

Corollary 3.7
.

Assume
S
is a K3 surface. Then for any Brauer class
\beta\in{\rm Br}(S^{[n]})
on the Hilbert scheme
S^{[n]}

one has

{\rm ind}(\beta)\mid{\rm per}(\beta)^{n}.

Proof.

The proof is similar to the one of Proposition
3.2
.
By virtue of the lemma, we can write any Brauer class
\beta
on the Hilbert scheme
S^{[n]}

as
\alpha^{[n]}
for some Brauer class
\alpha
on the surface
S
. Using de Jong’s solution of the period-index problem for surfaces, see
[
16
]
, or the alternative proof for complex K3 surfaces in
[
32
]
, we know that
\alpha
can be represented by an Azumaya algebra
{\cal A}
on
S
of index
{\rm ind}({\cal A})={\rm rk}({\cal A})^{1/2}={\rm per}(\alpha)
.

Let us denote by
{\cal B}
the pull-back of the exterior product
{\cal A}\boxtimes\cdots\boxtimes{\cal A}
on
S^{n}
under the blow-up morphism
p\colon{\rm Bl}(S^{n})\coloneqq S^{[n]}\times_{S^{(n)}}S^{n}\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces S^{n}
.
Then
{\cal B}
is an Azumaya algebra on
{\rm Bl}(S^{n})
which represents the class
p^{\ast}\alpha^{\times n}
.
Clearly,
{\cal B}
is naturally linearised with respect to the natural
{\mathfrak{S}}_{n}
-action
on
{\rm Bl}(S^{n})
. Thus, on the open set where the action is free, i.e. the complement of the big diagonal in
S^{n}
, the Azumaya algebra
{\cal B}
descends to an Azumaya algebra on the complement
U\coloneqq S^{[n]}\,\setminus\,E
of the exceptional locus
E\subset S^{[n]}
of the Hilbert–Chow morphism. Hence,
\beta|_{U}

is represented by an Azumaya algebra of rank
{\rm rk}({\cal A})^{n}={\rm per}(\alpha)^{2n}
and, therefore,

{\rm ind}(\beta)\mid{\rm per}(\alpha)^{n}
. Since
{\rm per}(\alpha)={\rm per}(\beta)
, this proves the result.
∎

Remark 3.8
.

The proof above only shows
{\rm ind}(\beta)\mid{\rm per}(\beta)^{n}
, but not that there actually exists
an Azumaya algebra
{\cal B}
on
S^{[n]}
whose index
{\rm ind}({\cal B})={\rm rk}({\cal B})^{1/2}
divides
{\rm per}(\beta)^{n}
. Examples of Brauer classes on a scheme not representable by an Azumaya
algebra that realises the index at the generic point have been studied before,
e.g. by Antieau and Williams
[
3
,
4
]
.

The same issue is the major obstacle to apply deformation theory, e.g. via hyperholomorphic
bundles and twistor spaces, to generalise our results to arbitrary hyperkähler manifolds.
To even start the process one would need a sheaf of Azumaya algebras or a locally free twisted
sheaf on all of
X
. But even when this can be guaranteed, one would still need to ensure
stability and control over the second Chern class.

3.5.
Surface decomposable hyperkähler manifolds

The Hilbert scheme
S^{[n]}
of a K3 surface
S
is rationally dominated
by the product of surfaces
S^{n}
and one could study more generally
the period-index problem on hyperkähler manifolds
X
that are
rationally dominated by a product of surfaces
\pi\colon S_{1}\times\cdots\times S_{n}\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces X
.

For Brauer classes
\alpha
on
X
that pull-back to product classes
\alpha_{1}\times\cdots\times\alpha_{n}
the solution of the period-index problem for surfaces
can again be applied to prove that
{\rm ind}(\alpha)\mid{\rm per}(\alpha)^{n}
at least for classes of a period coprime
to the degree of
\pi
. However, in general, the pull-back might involve terms coming from
H^{1}(S_{i})\otimes H^{1}(S_{j})
. This does not happen for the Hilbert scheme, as the
pull-back of the two-form on
S^{[n]}
under the rational map
S^{n}\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces S^{[n]}
decomposes
into two forms on the factors. This gives an alternative argument to prove Corollary
3.7

but only for classes with
{\rm per}(\alpha)
coprime to
n!
.

Voisin
[
58
]
introduced the notion of surface decomposable varieties.
Instead of dominating rational maps from products of surfaces she allows dominating correspondences

\textstyle{S_{1}\times\cdots\times S_{n}}
\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\Gamma}
\textstyle{X,}

but imposes the condition that the pull-back of
H^{2,0}(X)
is a linear combination of
pull-backs of classes in
H^{2,0}(S_{i})
. Whenever a hyperkähler admits such a surface decomposition, then the period-index problem in the sense
of Conjecture
0.2
can be answered in the affirmative at least for those Brauer classes
with period coprime to the degrees of the two projections and the order of the torsion of
H^{3}(X,\mathbb{Z})
.

This can also be applied to generalised Kummer varieties
K_{n}(A)
, the second explicit series of examples
of hyperkähler manifolds.

Corollary 3.9
.

Let
X=K_{n}(A)
be a generalised Kummer variety of dimension
2n
and
\alpha\in{\rm Br}(X)
. If
{\rm per}(\alpha)
coprime to
(n+1)!
, then

{\rm ind}(\alpha)\mid{\rm per}(\alpha)^{n}.

Proof.

The assertion follows from the fact that
K_{n}(A)
is rationally dominated by a map

A^{n}\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces K_{n}(A)
of degree
(n+1)!
which has the necessary property, namely that
H^{2,0}(K_{n}(A))

pulls back to the direct sum
\bigoplus p_{i}^{\ast}H^{2,0}(A)
.
∎

Potentially, a descent argument as in the proof of Corollary
3.7

could be used to get rid
of the coprimality condition on
{\rm per}(\alpha)
. However,
{\cal A}\boxtimes\cdots\boxtimes{\cal A}
on
A^{n}
is a priori no longer
linearised with respect to the relevant group action, here of
{\mathfrak{S}}_{n+1}
. But one could try to restrict

{\cal A}\boxtimes\cdots\boxtimes{\cal A}
on
A^{n+1}
to
A^{n}\,\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@hook{1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces A^{n+1}
,
(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@stopper}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n},-\sum x_{i})
, which then descends to
K_{n}(A)
on a dense open subset. This would
eventually prove
{\rm ind}(\alpha)\mid{\rm per}(\alpha)^{n+1}
for all Brauer classes

\alpha\in{\rm Br}(K_{n}(A))
on the
n
-dimensional hyperkähler manifold
K_{n}(A)
. We leave the details to the reader.

3.6.
Hilbert schemes of elliptic K3 surfaces

Assume now that the K3 surface
S

comes with a genus one fibration
S\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\mathbb{P}^{1}
. Then its symmetric product
S^{[n]}

inherits a Lagrangian fibration
S^{[n]}\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces(\mathbb{P}^{1})^{[n]}\simeq\mathbb{P}^{n}
.

According to Theorem
0.4
(or Corollary
3.7
), we have
{\rm ind}(\alpha)\mid{\rm per}(\alpha)^{n}

for all
\alpha\in{\rm Br}(S^{[n]})
, while Theorem
0.3
only ensures it for
classes
\alpha\in{\rm Br}(S)
with a period coprime to

N_{S}\coloneqq\deg(S/\mathbb{P}^{1})\cdot\text{disc}({\rm NS}(S)).

Here,
\deg(S/\mathbb{P}^{1})
is the minimal degree of a multi-section
C\subset S
of
S\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\mathbb{P}^{1}
.

Indeed, a multi-section
C/\mathbb{P}^{1}
of degree
m
of
S\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\mathbb{P}^{1}
induces a multi-section

C^{[n]}/(\mathbb{P}^{1})^{[n]}
of degree
m^{n}
of
S^{[n]}\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\mathbb{P}^{n}
. For
S\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\mathbb{P}^{1}
the minimal degree of a multi-section coincides with the minimal degree of a relative polarisation.
Thus, in the definition of
N_{X}
for
X=S^{[n]}
, cf. (
0.1
) and §​
2.5
, the factors
\deg({\cal L})
and
\deg(\tilde{\mathbb{P}}/\mathbb{P})

contribute with the same prime factors. Since,
H^{3}(S^{[n]},\mathbb{Z})
is torsion free, cf. Remark

2.1
, the only
other contributing factor in the definition of
N_{X}
is the index of

\left(T(S^{[n]})_{0}\subset H^{2}(S^{[n]},\mathbb{Z})/{\rm NS}(S^{[n]})\right)\simeq\left(T(S)\subset H^{2}(S,\mathbb{Z})/{\rm NS}(S)\phantom{{}^{[n]}\!\!\!\!\!\!}\right)

which
is just
\text{disc}({\rm NS}(S))
. Here we use that for a K3 surface
T(S)\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 14.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces H^{0}(\mathbb{P}^{1},R^{2}h_{\ast}\mathbb{Z})
is trivial.

We view this as further evidence, that eventually the condition in Theorem
0.3
of
{\rm per}(\alpha)
being coprime to
N_{X}
should be superfluous.
